216:## S11. Retention sweep: DeleteExpired / DeleteProfile wiring (LT-44)
217-
218-**Description:** Consistency-pass finding F21 identified that 05's retention policy (`pii-governance.md`) had no reachable DAO method, and no track owned writing the sweep job itself. 05's contract addendum (`multi-db-strategy.md` §3c, post-Oren-review-final) added `DeleteExpired(ctx, class, olderThan, maxRows)` (batched, `SweepResult{RowsExamined, RowsDeleted, OldestSurvivingAt, Drained}`, one transaction per batch) and `DeleteProfile(ctx, id, externalRef)` (reason is asserted internally as `"subject_request"`, not caller-supplied) to the composite — this story is 03 claiming ownership of the code that calls them: the periodic batched sweep invocation (looping per `RetentionClass` until `Drained`, using `pii-governance.md`'s windows and a bounded `maxRows` per call), the subject-deletion request path, and surfacing the health signal to 04.
219-
220-**Acceptance criteria:**
221-- [ ] Implements 05's addendum without re-deriving the transaction-boundary or batching reasoning.
222-- [ ] Loops `DeleteExpired` per `RetentionClass` until `Drained` is true, each using the class's own clock as specified (`updated_at` for `direct`/`idp_cache`, `created_at` for orphans) — enforcement site: the sweep invocation loop, one class at a time, no shared/mixed predicate.
223-- [ ] `DeleteExpired` is never called with a request-scoped context — the sweep's own job/scheduler context governs it, never an inbound HTTP request's, so a sweep is never silently cancelled because an unrelated request ended — enforcement site: the sweep invocation code's context construction, plus a test asserting an already-cancelled context returns before deleting anything.
224-- [ ] `SweepResult.OldestSurvivingAt` is published to 04's observability **only from a `Drained == true` result** — a non-drained (mid-sweep) batch reports progress, not health, and must not fire the alert; 04 alerts only from the drained result (flagged to Theo directly, since this is the interaction Priya caught that neither original finding raised).
225-- [ ] `DeleteProfile` is the only path used for a subject-deletion request; ordinary application deletes still go through `Profiles().Delete` (no log row), and this distinction is preserved rather than collapsed for convenience.
226-- [ ] The `Drained`/`OldestSurvivingAt` coupling gets a one-line doc comment at the field declaration in the eventual Go code (Oren's note) — so a future implementer reading `OldestSurvivingAt` in isolation mid-sweep doesn't treat a stale value as current.
227-
228-**Depends on:** S2; **05: retention sweep contract addendum** (`multi-db-strategy.md` §3c, pending Oren's first-pass review — same process as the original contract); **04: Story 5 — Observability** (`04-infra-devops/backlog.md`, owns where the sweep job runs, and the `rows-examined`/`rows-deleted`/`oldest-surviving-row-age` metrics sink and alert rule this story's `OldestSurvivingAt` feeds). Named as Story 5, not Story 6 — Story 6 is log-sink retention for the audit trail, a different concern; Story 5 is the one that actually specifies the retention-sweep alerting this story depends on.
229-**Model/effort:** sonnet, high.
230-**Type:** Story.
231-**Labels:** `track-03`, `from-consistency-pass`, `security-graded`, `no-code-yet`.

33:| `user_profile`, `source='idp_cache'` | `updated_at` | **30 days** since last hydration | Engineering: **a cache is not a record.** Re-hydration costs one `/identity` call, so deleting too early costs a vendor round-trip. Vendor data also goes stale — a 14-month-old cached address is a correctness problem before it is a privacy one. We are downstream custodian of another party's collected PII with no independent relationship to the subject, which argues for the shortest window that still functions | Hard delete | Cascade FK; a cached profile should not normally have credentials — if it does, it is no longer merely a cache and product must say which clock wins | unknown | Proposed |
34:| `user_profile`, `source='idp_cache'`, **no referencing `user_credential`** (orphaned cache) | **`created_at`**, deliberately not `updated_at` | **7 days** since creation | Engineering: lookup residue, not an account — someone called `/identity`, we cached the answer, nothing was ever built on it. **The clock is `created_at` because `updated_at` is touched by the read and re-hydration path: an orphan re-read on any schedule would keep resetting its own expiry and never die.** Closing that loop is the entire reason this row exists | Hard delete | Nothing — by definition it has no children | unknown | Proposed |
38:**The audit-stream row is enforced by 04, not by this schema.** Its enforcement site is 04's log-sink retention policy (`../04-infra-devops/observability.md`), which is what the consistency pass ruled and what `LT-20` cites. Nothing in the DAO reaches it: the sweep methods in `multi-db-strategy.md` §3c operate on `RetentionClass` values, all three of which are `user_profile` classes.
43:2. **No method sweeps it.** `DeleteExpired` takes a `RetentionClass`, and there is no class for it. If a window is ruled, implementing it is a **contract change** — either a fourth class or a separate method — not a configuration value someone can set. Flagging that now so the cost is visible at the time of the ruling rather than discovered after it.
60:| **Bulk retention sweep** (scheduled) | `source` = the class, plus that class's clock older than that class's window; orphan class additionally requires no referencing credential. **One class per pass — never a mixed-class sweep**, because a single predicate spanning two clocks is how the wrong window gets applied to the wrong rows | Many rows | Cascade delete, implicit and unmentioned in the predicate — the sweep selects profiles and destroys credentials as a side effect. Stated because it is invisible at the point of use | **Hard delete** | One row in a separate `deletion_log`: profile UUID, `source`, `deleted_at`, reason `retention_sweep`, job run id. **No name, phone or address** | Schedule it; run it per class; emit the metrics below | **Nothing errors.** No exception, no alert, no symptom — rows accumulate and the policy is fiction nobody can see is fiction |

25	
26	## Retention windows (S5)
27	
28	**Every number below is a proposed POC default pending a product/legal ruling.** One asymmetry belongs stated plainly rather than buried: GDPR Art. 5(1)(e) storage limitation is a **principle** — data kept "no longer than is necessary" — demanding a window someone justifies. It is not a number anyone can look up. So every basis below is either an engineering rationale or the honest word *unknown*. There is no third kind, and a citation implying a regulation supplies a specific duration would be a false statement in a client-facing document.
29	
30	| Data class | Clock column | Proposed window | Basis | Disposal | What cascades | Ratifier | Status |
31	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
32	| `user_profile`, `source='direct'` | `updated_at` | **24 months** since last update | *unknown — needs product/legal ruling.* Engineering basis only: long enough that a dormant account surviving a slow re-engagement cycle is not destroyed, short enough to be a defensible answer to "why do you still hold this." No regulation supplies this number | Hard delete | Cascade FK destroys all `user_credential` rows — the account stops existing | unknown — no named legal owner on this POC | Proposed |
33	| `user_profile`, `source='idp_cache'` | `updated_at` | **30 days** since last hydration | Engineering: **a cache is not a record.** Re-hydration costs one `/identity` call, so deleting too early costs a vendor round-trip. Vendor data also goes stale — a 14-month-old cached address is a correctness problem before it is a privacy one. We are downstream custodian of another party's collected PII with no independent relationship to the subject, which argues for the shortest window that still functions | Hard delete | Cascade FK; a cached profile should not normally have credentials — if it does, it is no longer merely a cache and product must say which clock wins | unknown | Proposed |
34	| `user_profile`, `source='idp_cache'`, **no referencing `user_credential`** (orphaned cache) | **`created_at`**, deliberately not `updated_at` | **7 days** since creation | Engineering: lookup residue, not an account — someone called `/identity`, we cached the answer, nothing was ever built on it. **The clock is `created_at` because `updated_at` is touched by the read and re-hydration path: an orphan re-read on any schedule would keep resetting its own expiry and never die.** Closing that loop is the entire reason this row exists | Hard delete | Nothing — by definition it has no children | unknown | Proposed |
35	| **Audit-log stream** (the emitted log entries evidencing deletions, as read by 04's sink) | the entry's own timestamp | **Proposed, pending ruling — see basis** | **unknown — needs a legal ruling.** The audit stream evidences that a deletion happened, which is useful for exactly as long as someone may ask. That period is a question about limitation windows and regulator expectations, not an engineering one, and no engineering rationale substitutes for it | Expiry at the sink | Nothing | unknown — needs product/legal ruling | **Proposed** |
36	| `user_credential` | — | **No independent window** | Wholly governed by its profile's window via the cascade FK. A credential cannot outlive its profile; a credential row with no profile cannot exist. A second clock could only ever disagree with the first, with no resolution rule | n/a | n/a | **Ruled** |
37	
38	**The audit-stream row is enforced by 04, not by this schema.** Its enforcement site is 04's log-sink retention policy (`../04-infra-devops/observability.md`), which is what the consistency pass ruled and what `LT-20` cites. Nothing in the DAO reaches it: the sweep methods in `multi-db-strategy.md` §3c operate on `RetentionClass` values, all three of which are `user_profile` classes.

33:## Retention-sweep observability (05's S5 requirement, final)
35:Per `../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`: the retention sweep must emit, per data class per run, three metrics — rows examined, rows deleted, and **the age of the oldest surviving row in that class**. An alert fires when that age exceeds the class's stated retention window plus one sweep interval. This is the one metric in this document tied to a specific reasoning worth repeating: a sweep job that silently stops running produces no error and no symptom on its own — "rows deleted" can't tell a broken sweep from a legitimately empty one, since both report zero. Oldest-surviving-row age is the one number that keeps climbing when the job is dead and holds steady when it's healthy, which is why it's the alerting signal, not just a nice-to-have metric alongside the other two.
37:Mechanism: the sweep job (whatever invokes it — a CronJob against the lab cluster, per 05's mechanism-agnostic requirement) emits these as Prometheus metrics with a `class` label matching 05's actual retention classes — `source="direct"`, `source="idp_cache"`, and `idp_cache_orphan` for an orphaned `idp_cache` row (`../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`), never a label carrying the PII itself, same discipline as the RED-metrics rule above. (An earlier draft of this section invented `vendor_token_cache`/`idp_identity_cache` as class names — those don't correspond to anything 05 tracks and described a connector token cache the S3 ruling never adopted; corrected here.) The alert rule is `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds{class="X"} > (retention_window_seconds + sweep_interval_seconds)`, evaluated per class.
39:**Batched sweeps: only a drained result feeds the metric.** 05's `DeleteExpired` is batched (a bounded `maxRows` per call, `SweepResult.Drained` marking the last batch of a sweep) — `OldestSurvivingAt` is meaningful only on a `Drained == true` result, since mid-sweep the oldest deletable row is still present by construction and would read as stale on every batch but the last. The sweep job emits `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds` only from drained results, never from an intermediate batch; the alert rule above already only ever sees what's published, so this is a constraint on what the job emits, not an extra filter the alert needs to apply. Getting this wrong means the alert fires on every sweep and gets muted, which is worse than not having it.
49:No SLOs/error-budget policy, no alerting-rule catalog beyond the retention-sweep alert above. A take-home needs the shape of an observability approach and a credible enforcement mechanism for the one hard requirement (never-log); it doesn't need a full SRE runbook.

This story is the **caller**: a scheduled invocation that loops per `RetentionClass` until drained, plus a subject-deletion request path, plus the health metric that tells 04 whether it's actually running.\n\n**Mechanism (team-lead's recommendation, adopted):** a `CronJob` in `04-infra-devops`'s manifests invoking `cmd/api-service`'s existing image in a new `--sweep` mode (or a distinct small entrypoint, `03`'s call) — not a new binary, not a new image build, reusing what's already deployed.\n\n## The three retention classes and windows this sweep must implement (from `pii-governance.md`, cited so this story doesn't re-derive them)\n\n| `RetentionClass` | Clock | Window | Why this clock specifically |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| `direct` | `updated_at` | 24 months | Proposed POC default, engineering basis only — explicitly **not** a legal ruling (`pii-governance.md`'s own \"unknown, needs product/legal ruling\" status). This story implements the number as configured, doesn't treat it as settled policy. |\n| `idp_cache` | `updated_at` | 30 days | A cache, not a record — re-hydration costs one `/identity` call, so the window trades staleness against re-fetch cost. |\n| `idp_cache_orphan` | `created_at`, **deliberately not `updated_at`** | 7 days | Using `updated_at` would let an orphan's own read/re-hydration path reset its expiry indefinitely — the entire reason this class exists as `created_at`-clocked, not a stylistic choice. |\n\n**These are stated as \"Proposed\" in `pii-governance.md`, not \"Ruled,\" except `user_credential`'s no-independent-window rule.** This story implements the configured numbers; it is not this story's job to decide whether 24 months/30 days/7 days are the right numbers — that's Priya's/legal's call, already flagged as open in the source document.\n\n## Acceptance criteria (restated from `backlog.md` S11)\n\n- [ ] Implements 05's addendum without re-deriving the transaction-boundary or batching reasoning (each `DeleteExpired` call is already one transaction per batch, per the DAO's own implementation).\n- [ ] Loops `DeleteExpired` per `RetentionClass` until `Drained` is true, one class at a time, never a mixed/shared predicate — enforcement site: the sweep invocation loop's own structure.\n- [ ] `DeleteExpired` is never called with a request-scoped context — the sweep's own job/scheduler context governs it. Enforcement site: the sweep invocation's context construction (the `CronJob`'s own pod lifecycle, not an inbound HTTP request), plus a test asserting an already-cancelled context returns before deleting anything (the DAO layer already has this test per LT-38; this story's own test confirms the *caller* doesn't accidentally pass a request-scoped one).\n- [ ] `SweepResult.OldestSurvivingAt` is published to 04's observability **only from a `Drained == true` result** — a non-drained batch reports progress, not health, per `observability.md`'s explicit warning that getting this wrong \"means the alert fires on every sweep and gets muted, which is worse than not having it.\" Enforcement site: the metrics-emission code path, gated on `Drained`.\n- [ ] Metrics emitted per class per run: `rows_examined`, `rows_deleted`, `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds{class=\"direct\"|\"idp_cache\"|\"idp_cache_orphan\"}` — matching `observability.md`'s exact label values (not the earlier-corrected, invented `vendor_token_cache`/`idp_identity_cache` names that document itself flagged and fixed).\n- [ ] `DeleteProfile` is the only path used for a subject-deletion request; ordinary application deletes still go through `Profiles().Delete` (no log row) — this distinction is preserved, not collapsed for convenience.\n- [ ] The `Drained`/`OldestSurvivingAt` coupling gets a one-line doc comment at the field declaration (Oren's note) — already present in the DAO's own code per LT-42's completeness sweep; this story doesn't need to add it again, just not remove or contradict it.\n- [ ] **The `CronJob` runs against `api-service`'s existing image** (a `--sweep` mode or distinct entrypoint), not a new image build — matching team-lead's stated \"half-day story\" framing; a new image/Dockerfile would be disproportionate scope for what this needs.\n- [ ] **Bounded by the existing `maxRows` per call** — the sweep doesn't invent a new batching parameter; it uses the DAO's own already-validated `1..10_000` bound.\n- [ ] **Audited** — every deletion this sweep performs writes exactly one `deletion_log` row via the DAO's own transaction (already guaranteed by the DAO layer per LT-38's test); this story's job is only to confirm it doesn't bypass that path.\n\n## Non-goals\n\n- No new DAO methods, no schema changes — `DeleteExpired`/`DeleteProfile` and `deletion_log` already exist and are tested.\n- No change to the retention windows themselves (24 months/30 days/7 days) — those are Priya's/legal's numbers, implemented as configured, not re-litigated here.\n- No new container image — reuses `api-service`'s existing image in a new invocation mode.\n- No subject-deletion-request *API endpoint* — `backlog.md`'s S11 wires the DAO-level `DeleteProfile` path; whether/how an external caller triggers a subject-deletion request (a support-tool button, an API route) is a separate, unscoped question not raised by this story's own criteria.\n\n## PO review script\n\n1. Another Claude session sent a message: <teammate-message teammate_id="tobias-lindqvist" color="yellow"> {"type":"idle_notification","from":"tobias-lindqvist","timestamp":"2026-09-13T16:47:14.516Z","idleReason":"available","result":"## Objection — LT-44 refinement\n\nNothing in this story's acceptance criteria or PO review script names the `CronJob`'s `concurrencyPolicy`. Kubernetes' default is `Allow` — if a sweep run takes longer than the schedule interval (plausible on the *first* real run, since this project has never swept before and there may be a real backlog across all three `RetentionClass`es, or under ordinary DB load), the next scheduled invocation starts anyway, and two sweep processes run concurrently, each looping \"until `Drained`\" against the same DAO methods. That's the same shape of problem this project already solved once, deliberately, for the migration `Job` in LT-45 (\"only one migration run can reach the migrate stage at a time, across concurrent deploys — enforced by the pipeline's own concurrency group/mutex\"). Nobody carried that discipline forward to this `CronJob`, even though the underlying risk — two schedulers' worth of work hitting the same tables and transaction paths concurrently — is structurally the same.\n\nIt's not just wasted DB load. Criterion 4 requires metrics published \"only from a `Drained == true` result,\" specifically because `observability.md` warns that getting this wrong makes the alert noisy and gets muted. Two overlapping sweep instances for the same class could each independently reach `Drained == true` at different times and each emit `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds` for the same class in the same rough window — not corrupting data, but producing exactly the kind of confusing, duplicate signal this criterion was written to prevent, undermining the one metric criterion 5's alert rule depends on being trustworthy.\n\n**Counter-proposal:** Add an acceptance criterion: \"The sweep `CronJob` sets `concurrencyPolicy: Forbid`, so a still-running sweep blocks its own next scheduled invocation rather than overlapping it — matching the single-in-flight discipline already applied to the migration `Job` in LT-45.\" Add a PO-script step: read the `CronJob` manifest directly and confirm `concurrencyPolicy: Forbid` is set (not the default `Allow`), and — since `Forbid` silently skips a run rather than queuing it — confirm a skipped run is itself visible in observability (e.g., a metric or log line noting the previous run was still active), so a sweep that's perpetually behind schedule doesn't fail silently either."} </teammate-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. 28:## Story 2 — Deliver secrets to both services per 02's inventory (LT-46)
29-
30-**Description:** Implement the Kubernetes-native `Secret` delivery mechanism in `./secrets-delivery.md`: file-mounted secrets keyed by name only in manifests, RBAC scoped per-service (and, for the JWT signing key, scoped to the issuing code path specifically, not `api-service`'s verification path), cluster-level encryption-at-rest for the `Secret` resource type, and the migration-runner credential's backend-specific provisioning (`CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm` rights on PostgreSQL only, nothing extra on CockroachDB, no privilege model on SQLite) kept distinct from the runtime service credential (DML-only, no DDL, per 05's R4).
31-
32-**Acceptance criteria:**
33-- [ ] 02 (the hand-off's author) confirms this implementation matches `handoff-04-secrets.md` v2's inventory without needing to re-explain any row.
34-- [ ] No `Secret` value appears in a manifest, image layer, build arg, or CI log — enforcement site: manifests reference names only; CI's `gitleaks` stage (Story 3).
35-- [ ] No ServiceAccount can `get`/`list` a `Secret` it doesn't mount — enforcement site: per-service `Role`/`RoleBinding` scoped to named `Secret`s.

{
  "file_path": "/Users/warrenthompson/Source/LoginID/04-infra-devops/refinement/LT-46.md",
  "content": "# LT-46 refinement — Story 2: secret delivery, re-scoped to Vault\n\n**Product Owner:** Naomi Voss (01). **Research team:** Imogen Hale, Desmond Okafor, Tobias Lindqvist. **Engineering:** Theo Bergman (04, manifests); provisioning: Amber (Vault itself); review: Marcus Ilori (02, Vault policies).\n\n**Who did the work:** Refined by Naomi Voss (PO); Warren's ruling on the re-scoped criterion.\n\n## Why this changed\n\n`backlog.md`'s original Story 2 criterion 5 required Kubernetes `Secret` resources encrypted at rest in etcd, enforced via cluster `EncryptionConfiguration` — a control-plane setting on Warren's own shared cluster (confirmed absent when checked directly: no encryption-provider config on the kube-apiserver). Escalated to Warren as accept-residual-or-enable-and-reverify; **his ruling: neither — move application secrets out of Kubernetes `Secret` objects entirely**, into the cluster's existing Vault, delivered via the Vault Agent Injector. This closes the original concern more completely than encrypting `Secret`s at rest would have (a Vault-delivered secret is never written to etcd as a `Secret` object in the first place, not merely encrypted once there) and reuses infrastructure that already exists on this cluster rather than requiring a control-plane change on a shared box outside this project's authority.\n\n## Re-scoped criterion (replaces the original etcd-encryption line)\n\nApplication secret material is not stored in etcd; delivered by Vault Agent with per-`ServiceAccount` Vault roles. Enforcement site: Vault policy per role, plus a namespace check that none of the six application `Secret`s exist.\n\n## The six application secrets this criterion covers (enumerated directly from the deployed manifests, not assumed)\n\n1. `api-service-jwt-signing-key` — the JWT signing private key, currently mounted only into `api-service-issuer` (LT-32's F13 fix).\n2. `postgres-superuser` — consumed only by the Postgres `StatefulSet` itself.\n3. `db-migrator-credential` — the migration-runner credential (DDL rights), consumed by the migration `Job`.\n4. `db-runtime-credential` — the runtime service credential (DML-only), consumed by `api-service`.\n5. `issuer-db-migrator-credential` — the issuer's own migration-runner credential (client-store schema).\n6. `issuer-db-runtime-credential` — the issuer's own runtime credential, consumed by `api-service-issuer`.\n\n**Accepted platform exceptions, named explicitly rather than silently grandfathered in as Kubernetes `Secret`s:**\n- `postgres-server-tls`, `loginid-takehome-tls` — both `cert-manager`-issued TLS certificates. `cert-manager`'s own design outputs a Kubernetes `Secret`; that's the standard integration pattern for this cluster (every other app on it uses the same mechanism per `decisions/deploy-path.md`), not an application secret this project introduced, and not in scope for the Vault migration.\n- **Open question, flagged rather than assumed either way:** `qa-client-credential` (the review-only QA client credentials from LT-51/LT-41's joint review) — is this an \"application secret\" that should also move to Vault, or an ops/review-tooling artifact that can reasonably stay a Kubernetes `Secret`? Not decided here; flagging for Theo/Marcus to rule during implementation, named so it isn't silently omitted from either list.\n\n## Acceptance criteria for this re-scoped piece\n\n- [ ] All six application secrets above are delivered via Vault Agent Injector sidecar/init-container pattern, with a distinct Vault role per consuming `ServiceAccount` — matching the existing per-service RBAC-scoping discipline this project already applies to Kubernetes `Secret`s (never one shared credential across services).\n- [ ] A namespace check (manual `kubectl get secret -n loginid-takehome` or a CI/audit script) confirms none of the six names above exist as Kubernetes `Secret` objects post-migration.\n- [ ] Vault policies are scoped per-role to exactly the path(s) that role's service needs — no policy grants broader read access than the specific secret(s) that `ServiceAccount` consumes, mirroring the existing \"no ServiceAccount can get/list a Secret it doesn't mount\" discipline (`backlog.md`'s own criterion 3, unaffected by this change).\n- [ ] The migrator/runtime credential separation (criterion 4, unaffected) is preserved under Vault — two distinct Vault paths/roles, not one shared secret with two consumers.\n- [ ] The `qa-client-credential` disposition (Vault or stays-as-Secret) is explicitly decided and stated, not left ambiguous.\n- [ ] Marcus (02) reviews the Vault policies before this is called Done — same review discipline already applied to every other security-graded surface in this project.\n\n## Non-goals\n\n- No change to the TLS-certificate delivery mechanism (`cert-manager` → Kubernetes `Secret` stays as-is) — explicitly out of scope, named as an accepted platform exception above.\n- No change to the CI runner's own RBAC (still zero grants on `secrets`, already correct and unaffected by where the secrets themselves live).\n- No re-litigation of which secrets exist or what they're for — this story is a delivery-mechanism change, not a secrets-inventory redesign.\n\n## PO review script\n\n1. Confirm Vault Agent Injector annotations/sidecars are present on each consuming Deployment/StatefulSet/Job.\n2. `kubectl get secret -n loginid-takehome` — confirm none of the six named application secrets appear; confirm the two named TLS-certificate exceptions and the `qa-client-credential` disposition are the only ones present (or absent, per whatever was decided).\n3. Confirm each Vault role's policy is scoped to exactly its consuming service's own secret path(s) — read the policy directly, not inferred from the manifest alone.\n4. Confirm Marcus's review of the Vault policies happened and is recorded.\n5. Once deployed: re-review live, per team-lead's own stated plan — confirm the running pods actually receive secret material via Vault Agent (a file mount from the injector sidecar), not a fallback to an old Kubernetes `Secret` left in place by mistake.\n\n## Research team review\n\n**Desmond Okafor — claims check:** [pending]\n\n**Tobias Lindqvist — objection:** [pending]\n\n**Naomi Voss — ruling:** [pending]\n"
}

Another Claude session sent a message: <teammate-message teammate_id="tobias-lindqvist" color="yellow"> {"type":"idle_notification","from":"tobias-lindqvist","timestamp":"2026-09-13T16:55:21.976Z","idleReason":"available","result":"## Objection — LT-46 refinement\n\nThis re-scope trades \"encrypt secrets at rest\" for \"don't write them to etcd at all\" — a real improvement, correctly reasoned — but it also introduces a new runtime dependency this project didn't have before: every pod that previously read a `Secret` already present at scheduling time now has to successfully authenticate to and fetch from Vault *at startup*, on every restart, every rollout, every redeploy. Nothing in this refinement states what happens when that dependency isn't met — Vault sealed, the injector unable to reach it, a Vault policy drifted out of sync with a `ServiceAccount` — and this project has already found, twice, that \"what happens when a required piece of config isn't there\" is exactly the class of gap that surfaces as a silent, hard-to-diagnose outage rather than a clean failure (the `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER`/`AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` gap, then the `AUTH_JWKS_URL` gap on the connector — both were \"config the code supports but nothing wired,\" found live, both fixed with an explicit \"fail loud, not silently degrade\" rule). This re-scope hands the same failure shape a new entry point — an unreachable or misconfigured Vault — without carrying that already-learned discipline forward.\n\nIt's also a shared-infrastructure coupling this project didn't have before: Vault on this cluster serves other real tenants (per the pattern established throughout this project — shared, not sandboxed). If Vault has an outage or a maintenance window for reasons entirely unrelated to this take-home, every one of this project's pods that needs to (re)start during that window is now affected, in a way a plain Kubernetes `Secret` never would be. That's a reasonable trade for the security benefit, but it's a trade this refinement doesn't name or plan for at all.\n\n**Counter-proposal:** Add an acceptance criterion: \"A pod whose Vault Agent init-container cannot authenticate or fetch its secret within a bounded timeout fails visibly (`CrashLoopBackOff` with a distinguishing log line or event, not an indefinite `Init` hang) — matching the fail-loud discipline already applied to `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER`/`AUTH_JWKS_URL`.\" Add a PO-script step: deliberately point one Deployment's Vault role at a nonexistent path (or otherwise force an injector failure) and confirm the pod's failure state is immediately visible via `kubectl get pods`/events, not silently retrying forever with no operator-visible signal."} </teammate-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. **Accepted platform exceptions, named explicitly rather than silently grandfathered in as Kubernetes `Secret`s:**
- `postgres-server-tls`, `loginid-takehome-tls` — both `cert-manager`-issued TLS certificates. `cert-manager`'s own design outputs a Kubernetes `Secret`; that's the standard integration pattern for this cluster (every other app on it uses the same mechanism per `decisions/deploy-path.md`), not an application secret this project introduced, and not in scope for the Vault migration.
- **Open question, flagged rather than assumed either way:** `qa-client-credential` (the review-only QA client credentials from LT-51/LT-41's joint review) — is this an "application secret" that should also move to Vault, or an ops/review-tooling artifact that can reasonably stay a Kubernetes `Secret`? Not decided here; flagging for Theo/Marcus to rule during implementation, named so it isn't silently omitted from either list.

func (s *Service) VerifyIdentity(ctx context.Context, vendorUsername, vendorPassword, phone, name string) (connector.Identity, error) {
	identity, err := s.attemptVerifyIdentity(ctx, vendorUsername, vendorPassword, phone, name)
	if errors.Is(err, ErrVendorCredentialRejected) {
		// A 401 from the connector is ambiguous from this seam's own
		// point of view: it means either OUR token or the vendor's was
		// rejected — idp-connector's own uniform-response-content
		// design (connector-security.md §1/F3) gives no way to tell
		// which. Invalidate the cached own-token and retry the WHOLE
		// flow exactly once with a freshly-minted one before concluding
		// the vendor credential itself was actually the problem — the
		// "refreshing ... on a 401" half of this story's caching
		// criterion.
		s.Tokens.Invalidate()
		identity, err = s.attemptVerifyIdentity(ctx, vendorUsername, vendorPassword, phone, name)
	}
	return identity, err
}

func (s *Service) attemptVerifyIdentity(ctx context.Context, vendorUsername, vendorPassword, phone, name string) (connector.Identity, error) {
	ourToken, err := s.Tokens.Token(ctx)
	if err != nil {
		// TokenSource.Token already logged this as its own distinct
		// failure class (a config/credential problem, not connector
		// downtime) — nothing further to add here.
		return connector.Identity{}, ErrIdentityVerificationUnavailable
	}

	vendorAccessToken, err := s.callAuth(ctx, ourToken, vendorUsername, vendorPassword)
	if err != nil {
		return connector.Identity{}, err
	}
	return s.callIdentity(ctx, ourToken, vendorAccessToken, phone, name)
}

func (s *Service) callAuth(ctx context.Context, ourToken, vendorUsername, vendorPassword string) (string, error) {
	callCtx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(ctx, connectorCallTimeout)
	defer cancel()

	var lastErr error
	for attempt := 0; attempt <= connectorMaxRetries; attempt++ {
		if attempt > 0 {
			select {
			case <-time.After(connectorRetryBackoff):
			case <-callCtx.Done():
				return "", ErrIdentityVerificationUnavailable
			}
		}
		token, err := s.Connector.Auth(callCtx, ourToken, vendorUsername, vendorPassword)
		if err == nil {
			return token, nil
		}
		if errors.Is(err, ErrVendorCredentialRejected) {
			// Not transient — retrying an actually-rejected credential
			// against an unreachable connector wouldn't help, and
			// retrying it against a reachable one would just waste
			// idp-connector's own rate-limit budget on a doomed
			// request. Surface immediately.
			return "", err
		}
		lastErr = err
	}
	log.Printf("onboarding: idp-connector /auth unreachable after %d attempts: %v", connectorMaxRetries+1, lastErr)
	return "", ErrIdentityVerificationUnavailable
}

func (s *Service) callIdentity(ctx context.Context, ourToken, vendorAccessToken, phone, name string) (connector.Identity, error) {
	callCtx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(ctx, connectorCallTimeout)
	defer cancel()

	var lastErr error
	for attempt := 0; attempt <= connectorMaxRetries; attempt++ {
		if attempt > 0 {
			select {
			case <-time.After(connectorRetryBackoff):
			case <-callCtx.Done():
				return connector.Identity{}, ErrIdentityVerificationUnavailable
			}
		}
		identity, err := s.Connector.Identity(callCtx, ourToken, vendorAccessToken, phone, name)
		if err == nil {
			return identity, nil
		}
		if errors.Is(err, ErrVendorCredentialRejected) {
			return connector.Identity{}, err
		}
		lastErr = err
	}
	log.Printf("onboarding: idp-connector /identity unreachable after %d attempts: %v", connectorMaxRetries+1, lastErr)
	return connector.Identity{}, ErrIdentityVerificationUnavailable
}

Key Technical Concepts: - Go backend: DAO layer (`internal/dao`, `postgres`/`sqlite`/`cockroachdb` packages), REST handlers (`internal/api`), connector (`internal/connector`), new onboarding-mediator package (`internal/onboarding`). - OAuth2 client-credentials grant (RFC 6749 §4.4), JWT (RS256, `kid` rotation, JWKS at `/.well-known/jwks.json`), Argon2id password/secret hashing with PHC format, timing-oracle mitigations. - Kubernetes: Deployments/StatefulSets/CronJobs, `NetworkPolicy` default-deny patterns, `RollingUpdate` vs `Recreate` strategy (`maxSurge` pitfall), ServiceAccount/RBAC scoping, `ConcurrencyPolicy: Forbid` for CronJobs, Vault Agent Injector (replacing raw K8s `Secret`s for app credentials). - CI/CD: GitHub Actions (`pr-check.yml`, `deploy.yml`), `t.Skip` vs `t.Fail` semantics, service containers for integration tests, `CI` env var gating. - Rate limiting/backoff: in-process vs. shared-store (Redis) limiters, per-replica state pitfalls, `GrantLimiter`/`RateLimiter`/`TouchCounter`/`identityRateLimiter` (keyed on `hashIdentity(callerSub, phone, name)`). - PII governance: `RetentionClass` (`direct`/`idp_cache`/`idp_cache_orphan`), `DeleteExpired`/`DeleteProfile`, `SweepResult{RowsExamined,RowsDeleted,OldestSurvivingAt,Drained}`, `deletion_log` audit table. - Security review patterns: never-log lists, fresh-header-per-outbound-request discipline, narrow-scoping of secrets to a single function's stack frame, fail-closed/fail-loud config validation, timing side-channel analysis. - Org process patterns: claims audit, structured written debate (position/rebuttal/fact-check/synthesis), rotating devil's advocate, newcomer's-question cold read, attack-tree red/blue review, independent second-review-with-git-log-verified-independence. - `git` multi-agent-safe practices: worktrees, command-line `-c user.name`/`-c user.email` overrides instead of persisted config in a shared tree. 3. 15	- REST search/retrieve handlers (LT-39 — Done), auth middleware (LT-40), real token issuance (LT-51 — refined, in progress), all verified live at `https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org` with real minted tokens.
16	- The connector's own inbound `/auth`/`/identity` contract (LT-41 — Done, verified in-cluster via port-forward with a real vendor stub backing it).
17	- CI/CD (LT-47), containerization (LT-45), secrets delivery (LT-46), database provisioning (LT-52) — all Done, deployed, live.
18	
19	**Deliberately stubbed, and why — this is the section a cold reader needs, stated plainly, not left to infer:**
20	- **The third-party vendor (ABC/XYZ) is an in-process `StubVendorClient`**, not a real HTTP integration — `idp-connector` falls back to it when `IDP_ABC_BASE_URL` is unset, logs this explicitly on startup, and this is `backlog.md`'s own stated non-goal for LT-41 ("no real vendor integration"). The assignment names the vendor generically; a real integration was never in scope.
21	- **The `authorize()` object-level policy hook is a `StopgapAuthorizer`**, not the real caller-referral datastore `handoff-03-auth.md`'s F8 resolution describes — a stated seam, not a hidden gap, per LT-39/LT-40's own non-goals.
22	- **CockroachDB and SQLite are exercised in CI only, never deployed live** — only Postgres runs in the actual cluster (LT-52's explicit scope limit). A reader should not expect to find CockroachDB running anywhere.
23	- **No `POST /profiles` write endpoint exists or is planned** — Q2 is search/retrieve by the assignment's own text; a write path was ruled out as scope creep (team-lead + Priya's decision, recorded on LT-39). Review/demo data enters via a version-controlled, idempotent SQL file (`deploy/demo-data/001-review-profiles.sql`) applied as a documented runbook step, not through the API.
24	- **Rate limiting, the touch counter, and the token-issuer's grant limiter are in-process, single-replica-only** — their own code comments state a shared store (Redis) is needed for a genuine multi-instance deployment; this project runs `api-service`/`api-service-issuer` at 1 replica with `Recreate` rollout strategy specifically so this simplification holds correctly, not silently degrading under a rolling update (a real live-tested bug this project found and fixed, not a hypothetical).
25	- **`api-service` calling the connector (LT-33)** is in progress as of this writing — the piece that makes Q2 and Q3 one system, not yet merged.
26	- **The retention sweep (LT-44) — designed and enforced at the schema/DAO level, not yet operationally running.** `DeleteExpired`/`DeleteProfile` exist on the DAO composite and are tested there, but nothing schedules or calls them; no cron/job wires the sweep to run against the live database. `pii-governance.md`'s retention policy is a real, tracked, backlogged story (S11/LT-44), not a silently-abandoned requirement — but a cold reader checking whether retention/deletion actually runs in production would otherwise have to discover its absence themselves.
27

1	# Track 03: Engineering & Delivery
2	
3	Go implementation of all three assignment questions — the DAO layer (Q1), the REST search/retrieve API with authentication (Q2), and the third-party identity connector (Q3) — plus the glue between Q2 and Q3. See root `../CLAUDE.md` for the full assignment text and how this track fits the other four; this file covers only what this track built, what's stubbed, and how.
4	
5	## What's implemented — live, deployed, verified against the running system
6	
7	- **Q1 — DAO layer** (`internal/dao`, `internal/dao/postgres`, `internal/dao/sqlite`): `user_profile`/`user_credential` storage behind one `Repository` interface, real implementations for Postgres/CockroachDB and SQLite. Verified against the actually-deployed Postgres database, and in CI against real Postgres and CockroachDB service containers (not mocked) — a cross-backend conformance suite (LT-38) asserts all three backends behave identically, not just that their Go signatures match.
8	- **Q2 — REST API** (`internal/api`, `internal/app`): `POST /profiles/search`, `GET /profiles/{id}`, and a real RFC 6749 client-credentials `POST /auth/token` grant, all behind RS256 JWT verification. Verified live at `https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org` with real minted tokens, including rate-limiting, masked-vs-full-read scoping, and pagination.
9	- **Q3 — connector** (`internal/connector`, `cmd/idp-connector`): the third-party IDP connector's own inbound `/auth`/`/identity` contract. Verified in-cluster via `kubectl port-forward` with a real vendor stub backing it — happy path, audience rejection, fail-closed identical-content error responses, a timing floor on the three internal failure branches, and a failure-ratio rate limiter actually firing under a live failed-auth burst, not just asserted in a unit test.
10	- **api-service → idp-connector integration** (`internal/onboarding`, LT-33): api-service's own outbound call to the connector, closing the loop between Q2 and Q3 — its own cached, refreshed OAuth2 client-credentials token; bounded timeouts and retry-with-backoff on connector calls; a generic upward failure on any connector-unreachable condition, with own-token-acquisition failures logged as a distinct class.
11	- CI/CD, containerization, secrets delivery, and database provisioning (04's stories, LT-45/46/47/52) are all Done and live — `pr-check` runs this track's tests against real Postgres and CockroachDB service containers on every PR, not just SQLite.
12	
13	## Deliberately stubbed — stated plainly, not left to infer
14	
15	- **The third-party vendor (ABC/XYZ) is an in-process `StubVendorClient`**, not a real HTTP integration. `idp-connector` falls back to it whenever `IDP_ABC_BASE_URL` is unset, and says so explicitly on startup. The assignment names the vendor generically — a real integration was never in scope (`refinement/LT-41.md`'s own stated non-goal).
16	- **The `authorize()` object-level policy hook is a `StopgapAuthorizer`**, a hard-coded, default-deny stand-in — not the real caller-referral datastore `../02-ai-security-architecture/handoff-03-auth.md`'s own design describes. A stated seam, not a hidden gap.
17	- **CockroachDB and SQLite are exercised in CI only, never deployed live.** Only Postgres runs in the actual cluster (LT-52's explicit scope limit) — don't go looking for a running CockroachDB instance anywhere.
18	- **No `POST /profiles` write endpoint exists or is planned.** Q2 is search/retrieve by the assignment's own text; a write path was ruled out as scope creep. Review/demo data enters via a version-controlled, idempotent SQL file (`deploy/demo-data/001-review-profiles.sql`), applied as a documented runbook step, never through the API.
19	- **Rate limiting, the touch counter, and the token issuer's grant limiter are in-process and single-replica-only.** Their own code comments say a shared store (Redis) would be needed for a genuine multi-instance deployment — this project runs `api-service`/`api-service-issuer` at 1 replica with a `Recreate` rollout strategy *specifically* so this simplification holds correctly rather than silently degrading under a rolling update. That constraint exists because of a real bug this project found live (see below), not as a hypothetical caveat.
20	- **The retention sweep (LT-44) is designed and enforced at the schema/DAO level, but not yet operationally running.** `DeleteExpired`/`DeleteProfile` exist on the DAO composite and are tested there directly; nothing yet schedules or calls them against the live database. `../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`'s retention policy is a real, tracked, backlogged story, not an abandoned requirement.
21	
22	## Package layout
23	
24	`cmd/api-service` and `cmd/idp-connector` are both thin wrappers — argument parsing and `main()` only. Route registration and config loading live once, in `internal/app` and `internal/config`, shared by both binaries rather than duplicated per binary (see `decisions/go-layout-debate.md` below). `internal/api` holds Q2's HTTP handlers, JWT middleware, rate limiting, and the token issuer; `internal/connector` holds Q3's inbound handlers and outbound vendor client; `internal/onboarding` holds LT-33's mediating client from `api-service` to the connector; `internal/dao` and its `postgres`/`sqlite` subpackages hold Q1, behind one `Repository` interface `internal/dao/conformance` tests identically across backends.
25	
26	## Test strategy
27	
28	Unit-heavy; integration deliberately stubbed where the assignment itself doesn't require full end-to-end coverage. Hand-written interface fakes, not generated mocks, for DAO/handler tests (`decisions/test-double-strategy.md`) — small enough interfaces that a fake struct per test case beats adopting a codegen dependency at this project's scale. The rule that any change to `dao.Repository` (or its sub-interfaces) updates its hand-written fake in the same PR is now a literal checklist line in `.github/pull_request_template.md`, not just prose in a design doc — LT-42's own review found the design doc alone insufficient (a PR landed an interface change without updating its fake, caught only in a later pass), so the checklist line is the actual enforcement site now, and the design doc explains why. Real-backend correctness (Postgres/CockroachDB/SQLite conformance, the CockroachDB serialization-retry path) is proven against real databases, in CI, not mocked — `internal/dao/conformance`'s own suite is the one enforcement site for "do the backends behave alike," distinct from a signature-matching test that proves nothing about behavior.
29	
30	## AI tooling and workflow
31	
32	Every implementation story in this track's backlog runs at `sonnet, high` except the connector (`idp-connector`'s `/auth`/`/identity`, Q3), which runs at `opus, high` — the one seat above sonnet in this track, per `../02-ai-security-architecture/planning-approach.md`'s connector-token-lifecycle exception: a subtle bug in that specific code path (a leaked or misscoped vendor token) directly contradicts this project's own threat model in a way most of this track's other code doesn't.
33	
34	Beyond model selection, implementation decisions in this track ran through structured adversarial review rather than being decided unilaterally:
35	- `decisions/go-layout-debate.md` — a three-hats debate (optimist/pessimist/pragmatist) on one binary vs. two, ruling to keep two binaries (already published and built against by 04) but merge the *authoring* cost via a shared `internal/app` package, plus a concrete requirement (bounded timeouts, no tight-looping retries on outbound calls) adopted from the pessimist's production-incident pattern-matching.
36	- `decisions/error-semantics.md` — a rotating devil's-advocate pass (Oren Castellan) that closed the sentinel-error set, forced a translation boundary so wrapped DB errors never leak driver text to an API client, and added explicit handling for `context.DeadlineExceeded`/`Canceled`.
37	- `decisions/context-propagation.md` — a second rotating devil's-advocate pass (Nolan Reyes, drawing on production-incident pattern-matching) that scoped multi-statement writes to a transaction under context cancellation, and named which write paths deliberately detach from the inbound context for durability.
38	- `decisions/test-double-strategy.md` — a third rotating devil's-advocate pass (Marisol Ferran) that weighed hand-written fakes against generated mocks; hand-written fakes stood, with the interface-drift risk closed procedurally (the PR-template checklist line above), not by adopting a codegen tool.
39	
40	This project is also a broader org-orchestration exercise — a PM agent coordinating five track leads, each running a small hired sub-team with deliberately varied personalities, using patterns like adversarial pairs, red/blue, and structured written debate (`../PLAN.md`, `../CASTING.md`) — the four decisions above are this track's own instance of that same structure, not a separate practice invented for engineering work alone.
41	
42	## Real, live-tested findings
43	
44	This track's own delivery process included live acceptance review against the actually-deployed system, not only code review and unit tests — and that process found and helped fix four real, distinct production-shaped bugs, each named individually rather than lumped together:
45	
46	1. **A CI coverage gap**, where `pr-check` silently *skipped* every Postgres and CockroachDB conformance test — only SQLite genuinely ran — while still reporting an overall green build. Fixed two ways: real Postgres/CockroachDB service containers added to the CI job, and every affected test fixture changed to fail loudly (not skip) when a required backend DSN is absent specifically in CI.
47	2. **A cross-replica rate-limiting bug** in `api-service`'s own resource-API rate limiter, reproduced by actually crossing its 10 req/min cap live against 2 running replicas — each replica's in-process limiter had no visibility into the other's request count, so the effective cap was silently doubled. Fixed by moving `api-service`/`api-service-issuer` to a single replica with a `Recreate` rollout strategy (04's story), a deliberate scope limit stated plainly above, not a silent workaround.
48	3. **`api-service`'s own Q2 token issuer/verifier wiring shipped with `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER`/`AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` unset** in its Kubernetes manifest — minted tokens carried no `iss` and were checked against an empty `aud`, "verifying" successfully for the wrong reason rather than failing loudly. Fixed in the manifest, plus a startup check (`cmd/api-service`) that now refuses to start with either unset, and a URL-shape check on the JWKS/issuer values specifically (a non-empty but garbage value is caught too, not just an absent one).
49	4. **A separate, later total outage in `idp-connector`'s (Q3's) own inbound auth** — its Deployment manifest never set `AUTH_JWKS_URL` at all, so every request, including a genuinely valid token, failed identically with a JWKS-fetch error. Distinct from finding 3: a different service, a different missing variable, found in a separate in-cluster review pass. Fixed the same way: the manifest, plus a startup check in `cmd/idp-connector` itself, plus a systemic test (`internal/config`'s manifest-vs-config test) that now parses every real Kubernetes Deployment manifest and asserts it sets everything its own mode requires — closing this specific class of gap structurally, in CI, rather than relying on a fourth live incident to catch the next occurrence.
50	
51	Naming these precisely — which subsystem, which variable, which fix — is itself part of what this AI-workflow note is meant to demonstrate: verification against the live, deployed system, not only against a test suite or a design doc.

package config_test

import (
	"os"
	"path/filepath"
	"runtime"
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"gopkg.in/yaml.v3"

	"loginid-takehome/internal/config"
)

// k8sManifestDoc is the minimal shape this test reads out of a
// Kubernetes Deployment manifest — just enough to find each container's
// image, its env var names, and (for APP_MODE specifically) their
// literal values. A generic map[string]any unmarshal would work too, but
// a typed struct makes the fields this test actually depends on explicit
// and greppable.
type k8sManifestDoc struct {
	Kind     string `yaml:"kind"`
	Metadata struct {
		Name string `yaml:"name"`
	} `yaml:"metadata"`
	Spec struct {
		Template struct {
			Spec struct {
				Containers []struct {
					Image string `yaml:"image"`
					Env   []struct {
						Name  string `yaml:"name"`
						Value string `yaml:"value"` // only ever read for APP_MODE; every other var's value is deliberately never inspected — some are secretKeyRef-sourced with no literal to read at all
					} `yaml:"env"`
				} `yaml:"containers"`
			} `yaml:"spec"`
		} `yaml:"template"`
	} `yaml:"spec"`
}

1	# Track 03: Engineering & Delivery
2	
3	Go implementation of all three assignment questions — the DAO layer (Q1), the REST search/retrieve API with authentication (Q2), and the third-party identity connector (Q3) — plus the glue between Q2 and Q3. See root `../CLAUDE.md` for the full assignment text and how this track fits the other four; this file covers only what this track built, what's stubbed, and how.
4	
5	## What's implemented — live, deployed, verified against the running system
6	
7	- **Q1 — DAO layer** (`internal/dao`, `internal/dao/postgres`, `internal/dao/sqlite`): `user_profile`/`user_credential` storage behind one `Repository` interface, real implementations for Postgres/CockroachDB and SQLite. Verified against the actually-deployed Postgres database, and in CI against real Postgres and CockroachDB service containers — `.github/workflows/pr-check.yml`'s `build-lint-test` job sets `TEST_POSTGRES_DSN`/`TEST_COCKROACHDB_DSN` and runs `go test ./...` against them directly; reproduce locally by setting those two env vars to a running Postgres/CockroachDB instance and running the same command (no docker-compose step of this repo's own — `pr-check.yml` uses GitHub Actions service containers). A cross-backend conformance suite (`internal/dao/conformance`, LT-38) asserts all three backends behave identically, not just that their Go signatures match.
8	- **Q2 — REST API** (`internal/api`, `internal/app`): `POST /profiles/search`, `GET /profiles/{id}`, and a real RFC 6749 client-credentials `POST /auth/token` grant, all behind RS256 JWT verification. Verified live at `https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org` with real minted tokens; the exact commands run are the PO review scripts in `refinement/LT-39.md`/`LT-40.md`/`LT-51.md`. Three distinct in-process controls, easy to conflate by name, so defined separately here: `internal/api.RateLimiter` (a per-minute cap per client credential — 60 req/min for read scopes, 10 req/min for search); `internal/api.TouchCounter` (a separate, 24-hour cumulative distinct-record-touch cap per client, aimed at slow enumeration rather than bursts); `internal/api.GrantLimiter` (backoff on repeated failed `/auth/token` grant attempts, keyed by client+source IP — a different mechanism from the two above, and also reused as-is by the connector, see Q3 below). All three are exercised live in the PO review scripts above.
9	- **Q3 — connector** (`internal/connector`, `cmd/idp-connector`): the third-party IDP connector's own inbound `/auth`/`/identity` contract. Verified in-cluster via `kubectl -n loginid-takehome port-forward svc/idp-connector 8081:443` with a real vendor stub backing it (`refinement/LT-41.md`'s own PO review script has the full command sequence) — happy path, audience rejection, fail-closed identical-content error responses, a timing floor on the three internal failure branches, and a second rate-limit dimension specific to this connector (`internal/connector.identityRateLimiter`, keyed by *which identity* was attempted, not just which caller — distinct from `api.GrantLimiter` above, which the connector also reuses for its caller+IP dimension) actually firing under a live failed-auth burst, not just asserted in a unit test.
10	- **api-service → idp-connector integration** (`internal/onboarding`, LT-33): api-service's own outbound call to the connector, closing the loop between Q2 and Q3 — its own cached, refreshed OAuth2 client-credentials token; bounded timeouts and retry-with-backoff on connector calls; a generic upward failure on any connector-unreachable condition, with own-token-acquisition failures logged as a distinct class.
11	- CI/CD, containerization, secrets delivery, and database provisioning (04's stories, LT-45/46/47/52) are all Done and live — `pr-check` runs this track's tests against real Postgres and CockroachDB service containers on every PR, not just SQLite.
12	
13	## Deliberately stubbed — stated plainly, not left to infer
14	
15	- **The third-party vendor (ABC/XYZ) is an in-process `StubVendorClient`**, not a real HTTP integration. `idp-connector` falls back to it whenever `IDP_ABC_BASE_URL` is unset, and says so explicitly on startup. The assignment names the vendor generically — a real integration was never in scope (`refinement/LT-41.md`'s own stated non-goal).
16	- **The `authorize()` object-level policy hook is a `StopgapAuthorizer`** (`internal/api/stopgap_authorizer.go`), a hard-coded, default-deny stand-in — not the real caller-referral datastore `../02-ai-security-architecture/handoff-03-auth.md`'s own design describes. Scope is narrow and stated in its own doc comment: it only gates `profile:read:own`'s referral-membership check (a hard-coded `sub → allowed record id` map, currently just the one seeded QA client); `profile:read:any` is authorized entirely upstream of it (a valid `reason_code`, checked in the handler before this hook ever runs), and `profile:search` has no per-record target for it to gate at all — both of those paths return `true` unconditionally here, not because the check is real, but because this stand-in has no real `caller_referral` data to consult for them. A stated seam, not a hidden gap.
17	- **CockroachDB and SQLite are exercised in CI only, never deployed live.** Only Postgres runs in the actual cluster (LT-52's explicit scope limit) — don't go looking for a running CockroachDB instance anywhere.
18	- **No `POST /profiles` write endpoint exists or is planned.** Q2 is search/retrieve by the assignment's own text; a write path was ruled out as scope creep. Review/demo data enters via a version-controlled, idempotent SQL file (`deploy/demo-data/review-profiles.sql`), applied under the database's `migrator` role as a runbook step documented in `refinement/LT-51.md` (item 9(a) of its joint review script), never through the API.
19	- **`RateLimiter`, `TouchCounter`, and `GrantLimiter`** (all three defined above, under Q2) **are in-process and single-replica-only.** Their own code comments say a shared store (Redis) would be needed for a genuine multi-instance deployment — this project runs `api-service`/`api-service-issuer` at 1 replica with a `Recreate` rollout strategy *specifically* so this simplification holds correctly rather than silently degrading under a rolling update. That constraint exists because of a real bug this project found live (see below), not as a hypothetical caveat.
20	- **The retention sweep (LT-44) is designed and enforced at the schema/DAO level, but not yet operationally running.** `DeleteExpired`/`DeleteProfile` exist on the DAO composite and are tested there directly; nothing yet schedules or calls them against the live database. `../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`'s retention policy is a real, tracked, backlogged story, not an abandoned requirement.
21	
22	## Package layout
23	
24	`cmd/api-service` and `cmd/idp-connector` are both thin wrappers — argument parsing and `main()` only. Route registration and config loading live once, in `internal/app` and `internal/config`, shared by both binaries rather than duplicated per binary (see `decisions/go-layout-debate.md` below). `internal/api` holds Q2's HTTP handlers, JWT middleware, rate limiting, and the token issuer; `internal/connector` holds Q3's inbound handlers and outbound vendor client; `internal/onboarding` holds LT-33's mediating client from `api-service` to the connector; `internal/dao` and its `postgres`/`sqlite` subpackages hold Q1, behind one `Repository` interface `internal/dao/conformance` tests identically across backends.
25	
26	## Test strategy
27	
28	Unit-heavy; integration deliberately stubbed where the assignment itself doesn't require full end-to-end coverage. Hand-written interface fakes, not generated mocks, for DAO/handler tests (`decisions/test-double-strategy.md`) — small enough interfaces that a fake struct per test case beats adopting a codegen dependency at this project's scale. The rule that any change to `dao.Repository` (or its sub-interfaces) updates its hand-written fake in the same PR is now a literal checklist line in `.github/pull_request_template.md`, not just prose in a design doc — LT-42's own review found the design doc alone insufficient (a PR landed an interface change without updating its fake, caught only in a later pass), so the checklist line is the actual enforcement site now, and the design doc explains why. Real-backend correctness (Postgres/CockroachDB/SQLite conformance, the CockroachDB serialization-retry path) is proven against real databases, in CI, not mocked — `internal/dao/conformance`'s own suite is the one enforcement site for "do the backends behave alike," distinct from a signature-matching test that proves nothing about behavior.
29	
30	## AI tooling and workflow
31	
32	Every implementation story in this track's backlog runs at `sonnet, high` except the connector (`idp-connector`'s `/auth`/`/identity`, Q3), which runs at `opus, high` — the one seat above sonnet in this track, per `../02-ai-security-architecture/planning-approach.md`'s connector-token-lifecycle exception: a subtle bug in that specific code path (a leaked or misscoped vendor token) directly contradicts this project's own threat model in a way most of this track's other code doesn't.
33	
34	Beyond model selection, implementation decisions in this track ran through structured adversarial review rather than being decided unilaterally:
35	- `decisions/go-layout-debate.md` — a three-hats debate (optimist/pessimist/pragmatist) on one binary vs. two, ruling to keep two binaries (already published and built against by 04) but merge the *authoring* cost via a shared `internal/app` package, plus a concrete requirement (bounded timeouts, no tight-looping retries on outbound calls) adopted from the pessimist's production-incident pattern-matching.
36	- `decisions/error-semantics.md` — a rotating devil's-advocate pass (Oren Castellan) that closed the sentinel-error set, forced a translation boundary so wrapped DB errors never leak driver text to an API client, and added explicit handling for `context.DeadlineExceeded`/`Canceled`.
37	- `decisions/context-propagation.md` — a second rotating devil's-advocate pass (Nolan Reyes, drawing on production-incident pattern-matching) that scoped multi-statement writes to a transaction under context cancellation, and named which write paths deliberately detach from the inbound context for durability.
38	- `decisions/test-double-strategy.md` — a third rotating devil's-advocate pass (Marisol Ferran) that weighed hand-written fakes against generated mocks; hand-written fakes stood, with the interface-drift risk closed procedurally (the PR-template checklist line above), not by adopting a codegen tool.
39	
40	This project is also a broader org-orchestration exercise — a PM agent coordinating five track leads, each running a small hired sub-team with deliberately varied personalities, using patterns like adversarial pairs, red/blue, and structured written debate (`../PLAN.md`, `../CASTING.md`) — the four decisions above are this track's own instance of that same structure, not a separate practice invented for engineering work alone.
41	
42	## Real, live-tested findings
43	
44	This track's own delivery process included live acceptance review against the actually-deployed system, not only code review and unit tests — and that process found and helped fix four real, distinct production-shaped bugs, each named individually rather than lumped together:
45	
46	1. **A CI coverage gap**, where `pr-check` silently *skipped* every Postgres and CockroachDB conformance test — only SQLite genuinely ran — while still reporting an overall green build. Fixed two ways: real Postgres/CockroachDB service containers added to the CI job, and every affected test fixture changed to fail loudly (not skip) when a required backend DSN is absent specifically in CI.
47	2. **A cross-replica rate-limiting bug** in `internal/api.RateLimiter` (the per-minute cap defined above under Q2, NOT `GrantLimiter` or `TouchCounter`), reproduced by actually crossing its 10 req/min search cap live against 2 running replicas — each replica's in-process limiter had no visibility into the other's request count, so the effective cap was silently doubled, and inconsistently (the exact overshoot depended on the load balancer's own distribution across pods). Fixed by moving `api-service`/`api-service-issuer` to a single replica with a `Recreate` rollout strategy (04's story), a deliberate scope limit stated plainly above, not a silent workaround. See `refinement/LT-39.md` for the full reproduction (both the pre-fix live burst test and the deterministic post-fix re-run).
48	3. **`api-service`'s own Q2 token issuer/verifier wiring shipped with `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER`/`AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` unset** in its Kubernetes manifest — minted tokens carried no `iss` and were checked against an empty `aud`, "verifying" successfully for the wrong reason rather than failing loudly. Fixed in the manifest, plus a startup check in `cmd/api-service/main.go` (`validateAuthConfig`) that now refuses to start with either unset, and a URL-shape check (`internal/config.ValidateEnvVarValue`) on the JWKS/issuer values specifically (a non-empty but garbage value is caught too, not just an absent one).
49	4. **A separate, later total outage in `idp-connector`'s (Q3's) own inbound auth** — its Deployment manifest never set `AUTH_JWKS_URL` at all, so every request, including a genuinely valid token, failed identically with a JWKS-fetch error. Distinct from finding 3: a different service, a different missing variable, found in a separate in-cluster review pass. Fixed the same way: the manifest, plus a startup check in `cmd/idp-connector/main.go` (`validateAuthConfig`), plus a systemic test (`internal/config/manifest_test.go`) that now parses every real Kubernetes Deployment manifest and asserts it sets everything its own mode requires — closing this specific class of gap structurally, in CI, rather than relying on a fourth live incident to catch the next occurrence.
50	
51	Naming these precisely — which subsystem, which variable, which fix — is itself part of what this AI-workflow note is meant to demonstrate: verification against the live, deployed system, not only against a test suite or a design doc.
52

--- stopgap ---
package api

import "context"

// StopgapAuthorizer is a TEMPORARY, default-deny Authorizer stand-in —
// NOT the real caller_referral-backed policy engine (handoff-03-auth.md
// v3's F8 resolution names that as a later story's own datastore work).
//
// Ruled by Marcus Ilori (02), in response to a direct question before
// this was wired to a live public URL: "A permissive allow-all
// Authorizer must never be deployed to a live public URL on a shared
// production cluster, 'temporary' or not — that's the exact failure this
// design exists to prevent, and it's a public repo, so being
// permissive-even-briefly is a permanent public fact." Ruling: default-
// deny, in-memory, hard-coded with a small fixed (sub, allowed record id)
// map for the seeded QA client credential (LT-51) — enough to
// demonstrate both the allow and deny paths of read:own's referral check
// in the live review, without ever allowing an arbitrary caller through.
//
// This only affects ScopeReadOwn's referral-membership check.
// ScopeReadAny's authorization is "has a valid reason_code," already
// checked in the handler before Authorize() is ever called; ScopeSearch
// has no per-record target for this Authorizer to gate at all. Both
// return true unconditionally here — there is no real caller_referral
// data this stopgap could consult for a real decision on those scopes
// either way, so denying them would not be more correct, only
// differently placeholder.
//
// A follow-up story replacing this with the real datastore-backed
// implementation must exist before this is considered load-bearing
// beyond the live-URL review — flag to the PM if it doesn't.
type StopgapAuthorizer struct {
	// ReadOwnAllow maps a caller sub to the set of record ids that sub
	// may read under ScopeReadOwn — the hard-coded "referral" set this
	// stopgap stands in for. Empty/nil means the map denies everything,
	// which is the safe default for any sub not explicitly seeded here.
	ReadOwnAllow map[string]map[string]bool
}

func (a *StopgapAuthorizer) Authorize(_ context.Context, sub string, scope Scope, target string) (bool, error) {
	switch scope {
	case ScopeReadOwn:
		allowed, ok := a.ReadOwnAllow[sub]
		if !ok {
			return false, nil
		}
		return allowed[target], nil
	case ScopeReadAny, ScopeSearch:
		return true, nil
	default:
		return false, nil
	}
}

1	# Hand-off: Vault provisioning for `loginid-takehome` (LT-46)
2	
3	**From:** Amber (OpenClaw Kubernetes agent on the Mac mini, model claude-opus-4-7), on Warren's authorization, 2026-09-13 10:06–10:25 PDT. **Requested by:** Dana Whitfield (PM). **Receiver:** Theo Bergman (04) for the manifest side; Helena Marsh (02) for policy review; Naomi Voss (01) for the refinement record.
4	
5	This is Amber's report, verbatim from her session output, lightly framed. It contains names and paths only; no secret value, token, or key appears here or appeared in her session. The request she answered is summarised at the end.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## 1. Kubernetes auth — already enabled, no change needed
10	
11	`auth/kubernetes` was already mounted and correctly configured for **this** cluster: `kubernetes_host = https://192.168.0.44:6443` (uplift-kub-ctrl-plane-1), `token_reviewer_jwt_set = true`, `disable_iss_validation = true`. I enabled nothing and re-configured nothing.
12	
13	## 2–3. KV mount and the copy
14	
15	**Mount `secret/`** — pre-existing and already KV **v2** (`options.version=2`), so I did not enable a new one. Prefix **`secret/loginid-takehome/`** (it joins existing `fmn/` and `mail/`).
16	
17	All six copied, key names preserved exactly, each at version 1:
18	
19	| Vault path | keys |
20	|---|---|
21	| `secret/loginid-takehome/db-runtime-credential` | `dsn`, `password` |
22	| `secret/loginid-takehome/db-migrator-credential` | `dsn`, `password` |
23	| `secret/loginid-takehome/issuer-db-runtime-credential` | `dsn`, `password` |
24	| `secret/loginid-takehome/issuer-db-migrator-credential` | `dsn`, `password` |
25	| `secret/loginid-takehome/api-service-jwt-signing-key` | `signing-key` |
26	| `secret/loginid-takehome/postgres-superuser` | `password` |
27	
28	Verified by SHA-256 per key, source vs. read-back — all identical. `postgres-server-tls`, `loginid-takehome-tls`, `qa-client-credential` untouched. **The Kubernetes Secrets were copied, not deleted** — nothing is removed until Theo's side is live and you say so.
29	
30	## 4–5. Policies and roles
31	
32	Five policies, `read` only, on exact `secret/data/loginid-takehome/<name>` paths; five `auth/kubernetes` roles of the same names, `token_ttl=1h`, `token_max_ttl=4h`, namespace `loginid-takehome`.
33	
34	| Role / policy name | bound ServiceAccount | read paths |
35	|---|---|---|
36	| `loginid-takehome-api-service` | `api-service` | db-runtime-credential, connector-client-credential |
37	| `loginid-takehome-api-service-issuer` | `api-service-issuer` | issuer-db-runtime-credential, api-service-jwt-signing-key |
38	| `loginid-takehome-db-migrate` | **`db-migrate`** | db-migrator-credential, issuer-db-migrator-credential |
39	| `loginid-takehome-postgres` | **`postgres`** | postgres-superuser + all four db credentials |
40	| `loginid-takehome-idp-connector` | **`idp-connector`** | none (policy exists, grants nothing) |
41	
42	`connector-client-credential` returns **404, not 403** for api-service — permitted by policy, just not populated yet. LT-33 can write it with no Vault change.
43	
44	## ⚠️ The blocker: three workloads have no ServiceAccount
45	
46	Your brief assumed each workload had its own `serviceAccountName`. It doesn't. Across the entire repo only `api-service` and `api-service-issuer` are defined. **`postgres` (StatefulSet), `db-migrate` (Job), and `idp-connector` (Deployment) declare no `serviceAccountName` and run as `default`** — confirmed against both the repo manifests and the live pods after Theo's 17:15Z redeploy.
47	
48	I did not bind three roles to `default`. Doing so would mean any pod on the `default` SA could assume **any** of those roles — `idp-connector`, which you ruled gets nothing, could have assumed `loginid-takehome-postgres` and read `postgres-superuser`. That would have inverted the least-privilege requirement while appearing to satisfy it.
49	
50	Instead I created dedicated ServiceAccounts `postgres`, `db-migrate`, `idp-connector` in the namespace and bound the roles to those. They're inert until used. Verified: `default` is denied all five roles (403), `idp-connector` cannot assume the postgres role (403), and the same SA name in another namespace is denied (403).
51	
52	**So Theo must add `serviceAccountName: postgres` / `db-migrate` / `idp-connector` to those three workloads.** Until he does, those three cannot authenticate to Vault at all — this is a hard failure, not a degradation. Ideally the SA objects go into `manifests.yaml` too so it's declarative; his `loginid-takehome-deployer` Role already has `serviceaccounts` create/update.
53	
54	## 6. Vault address for the injector
55	
56	**`https://vault.vault.svc:8200`** — note **https**, not the `http://` in your brief. The listener has `tls_disable = 0`. Theo does **not** need to set an address annotation: that exact value is already the injector's built-in default (`AGENT_INJECT_VAULT_ADDR`).
57	
58	For TLS, two options:
59	- **House pattern** (what ns `mail` uses): `vault.hashicorp.com/tls-skip-verify: "true"`.
60	- **Proper verification**: I staged Secret **`vault-ca-cert`** (key `ca.crt`) into `loginid-takehome`, fingerprint-identical to the one in ns `vault`. Mount via `vault.hashicorp.com/agent-extra-secret: "vault-ca-cert"` + `vault.hashicorp.com/ca-cert`. The cert's SANs include `vault.vault.svc`, so this validates cleanly.
61	
62	**Flag:** that CA expires **2026-12-14**, about three months out. Not urgent, not mine to renew unilaterally, but it shouldn't be a surprise.
63	
64	## End-to-end proof, and one trap for Theo
65	
66	I ran a throwaway pod through the real injector on the `api-service` SA and deleted it afterwards. Full chain works: SA → k8s auth → role → policy → KV v2 → agent → file.
67	
68	It also caught something worth having: the obvious multi-line template renders a **trailing newline** into the file — 49 bytes for a 48-byte password. A `\n` on a DB password fails auth looking exactly like a wrong password. The single-line form produces an exact byte match, and I verified this rather than just recommending it:
69	
70	```
71	'{{- with secret "secret/data/loginid-takehome/db-runtime-credential" -}}{{ .Data.data.password }}{{- end -}}'
72	```
73	
74	## What I changed beyond the brief
75	
76	- Created ServiceAccounts `postgres`, `db-migrate`, `idp-connector` (reason above).
77	- Created Secret `vault-ca-cert` in `loginid-takehome`.
78	- Nothing enabled, nothing deleted, no existing Vault policy or role touched. The four `db-*`/`issuer-*` credentials, the JWT key, and the superuser remain live as Kubernetes Secrets.
79	
80	No secret value, token, or key appears above or in any file I wrote; the local Vault token cache was shredded and the port-forward is closed.
81	
82	---
83	
84	## The request Amber answered (summary)
85	
86	Copy six named Kubernetes Secrets from `loginid-takehome` into KV v2 at `secret/loginid-takehome/<secret-name>` preserving key names; create five read-only policies and five Kubernetes-auth roles (`loginid-takehome-{api-service, api-service-issuer, db-migrate, postgres, idp-connector}`) bound to the matching ServiceAccounts in the namespace, `token_ttl=1h`, `token_max_ttl=4h`; report names only. Leave `postgres-server-tls`, `loginid-takehome-tls`, and `qa-client-credential` as Kubernetes Secrets by ruling (`../02-ai-security-architecture/handoff-04-secrets.md` v3.1).

104-## Story 6 — Configure log-sink retention for the audit trail (LT-20)
105-
106-**Description:** The consistency pass (`../decisions/cross-track-consistency.md`, "Deletion gap") ruled the enforcement site: a subject-deletion request reaches `user_profile`/`user_credential` by cascade but not application logs, metrics, or the audit trail, none of which has a `source` column or a deletion hook. 02's never-log list is the upstream control (PII values never enter a log in the first place, per `./observability.md`'s enforcement mechanism), which leaves exactly one remaining artifact needing a retention window: the audit trail itself (`sub`, scope, opaque record id, timestamp) — pseudonymous, not PII-free, and after a profile deletion the only remaining record that a given record id was looked up by a given caller. The enforcement site for its expiry is a retention policy on the log sink 04 already operates (Loki or equivalent, per `./observability.md`'s "Audit-log access" section) — not a deletion job, not a DAO method. 05 adds one row to `pii-governance.md`'s retention table for the audit-log window (basis: unknown, pending legal); 02 adds the two vocabulary/list fixes the pass identified for the never-log list (driver-error detail, `username`). This story configures the sink's retention setting to that window once it's a number.
107-

110-- [ ] The audit-log stream expires no later than 05's stated window — enforcement site: the log sink's retention configuration (a Loki `retention_period` or equivalent for the chosen sink), not application code.
111-- [ ] The retention configuration is separate from, and does not touch, any other log stream's retention or any Secret-access RBAC (`./secrets-delivery.md`) — enforcement site: sink-level retention is scoped per log stream/index, not global.
112-- [ ] Confirmed this is a configuration value, not a deletion job: no new DAO method, no new `cmd/` entrypoint, no scheduled job beyond what `./observability.md`'s retention-sweep story (Story 5) already covers for `user_profile`/`user_credential`-derived data.
