1	# Observability + Never-Log Enforcement
2	
3	Design-only. Covers logging, metrics, and tracing for both services, and — the harder half — how `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list gets enforced as a mechanism, not just stated as a policy.
4	
5	## Logging
6	
7	Structured logging (`slog`, Go stdlib — no third-party logging library needed for two services this size), JSON output, one line per event. Every log line that touches a request carries `sub` (caller identity), the endpoint, status, and a request id — never the request or response body wholesale. This is the pattern the never-log list requires (hand-off item 8: "full request bodies of `POST /auth` and `POST /identity`... both contain items above by construction") — the enforcement mechanism is that handlers log a small, explicit field set assembled by hand, never `log.Printf("%+v", req)` or equivalent reflection-based dumps of a request/response struct. A struct that contains a password field can't leak through a log statement that never receives the struct.
8	
9	**Enforcement beyond "write it carefully":**
10	- A lint rule (a `golangci-lint` custom check, or a simple `go vet`-style grep in CI) that flags any log call passing a whole request/response struct, a `*http.Request` body, or any variable named/typed to match the never-log list's fields (`password`, `token`, `secret`, `dsn`, `client_secret`). Not foolproof — a determined developer can rename around it — but it catches the accidental case, which is the common one.
11	- Error-path discipline: wrapped errors are logged with `err.Error()` truncated/sanitized where the error type is known to carry sensitive detail (a DB driver error can echo the DSN; a vendor HTTP client error can echo the response body per hand-off item 4) — the wrapping layer strips those before the error reaches a log call, not the log call itself deciding case-by-case.
12	
13	## Metrics
14	
15	Standard RED metrics (rate, errors, duration) per endpoint, via Prometheus client library, scraped by whatever the lab cluster already runs (not stood up new for this take-home). Labels: method, route, status class — never a label with cardinality tied to user data (no `sub` or phone number as a metric label; that turns a metrics backend into an accidental PII store with no access control at all, which is a worse leak than a log line since metrics systems are typically more widely readable).
16	
17	## Tracing
18	
19	Not built out here — a two-service take-home with no running collector doesn't get more credible by naming OpenTelemetry. If ever added, it follows the same field discipline as logging.
20	
21	## The never-log list, operationalized
22	
23	Hand-off's nine items map to two enforcement surfaces, not nine separate mechanisms:
24	
25	1. **Application-level:** the structured-logging discipline above (explicit field sets, the lint check, error-wrapping sanitization) covers items 1, 2, 5, 6, 7 (password, tokens, JWTs, client secrets, DSN) — these never enter a Go `log`/`slog` call in the first place.
26	2. **Proxy/ingress-level:** item 9 ("search query parameters containing a name or phone fragment... redact or hash before any access log, including proxy and ingress logs 04 controls") is the one item application code can't fully own, since ingress access logs are written by the ingress controller, not the service. Mechanism: an ingress-level log-format override that logs the request path with query string stripped (or a regex redaction on the `phone`/`name` query params specifically) rather than the default full-URL access log format. This is a one-time ingress ConfigMap change, not a per-request code path.
27	3. Item 3 (PII field values from `/identity` or `user_profile`) and item 4 (raw vendor error bodies) are the same discipline as #1 applied to the connector specifically — the connector logs "called vendor X, got status Y," never the response body.
28	
29	## Audit-log access is a separate grant from Secret access
30	
31	Per the hand-off's Kubernetes-specific requirements: the log stream carrying `sub`/scope/record-id/timestamp (the "fact of access" the never-log list still permits) is itself sensitive enough to reconstruct who looked up whom, so it gets its own RBAC — a distinct, narrower `Role` bound to an incident-review group, separate from any ServiceAccount's `Secret`-read grant (`./secrets-delivery.md`). Mechanism: if logs go to a cluster-local sink (e.g., Loki), that sink's read API gets its own RBAC/auth in front of it, not the same one guarding `kubectl get secrets`; if logs go to an external platform, the equivalent is a scoped read-only role in that platform, provisioned separately from any cluster credential.
32	
33	## Retention-sweep observability (05's S5 requirement, final)
34	
35	Per `../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`: the retention sweep must emit, per data class per run, three metrics — rows examined, rows deleted, and **the age of the oldest surviving row in that class**. An alert fires when that age exceeds the class's stated retention window plus one sweep interval. This is the one metric in this document tied to a specific reasoning worth repeating: a sweep job that silently stops running produces no error and no symptom on its own — "rows deleted" can't tell a broken sweep from a legitimately empty one, since both report zero. Oldest-surviving-row age is the one number that keeps climbing when the job is dead and holds steady when it's healthy, which is why it's the alerting signal, not just a nice-to-have metric alongside the other two.
36	
37	Mechanism: the sweep job (whatever invokes it — a CronJob against the lab cluster, per 05's mechanism-agnostic requirement) emits these as Prometheus metrics with a `class` label matching 05's actual retention classes — `source="direct"`, `source="idp_cache"`, and `idp_cache_orphan` for an orphaned `idp_cache` row (`../05-data-ops/pii-governance.md`), never a label carrying the PII itself, same discipline as the RED-metrics rule above. (An earlier draft of this section invented `vendor_token_cache`/`idp_identity_cache` as class names — those don't correspond to anything 05 tracks and described a connector token cache the S3 ruling never adopted; corrected here.) The alert rule is `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds{class="X"} > (retention_window_seconds + sweep_interval_seconds)`, evaluated per class.
38	
39	**Batched sweeps: only a drained result feeds the metric.** 05's `DeleteExpired` is batched (a bounded `maxRows` per call, `SweepResult.Drained` marking the last batch of a sweep) — `OldestSurvivingAt` is meaningful only on a `Drained == true` result, since mid-sweep the oldest deletable row is still present by construction and would read as stale on every batch but the last. The sweep job emits `oldest_surviving_row_age_seconds` only from drained results, never from an intermediate batch; the alert rule above already only ever sees what's published, so this is a constraint on what the job emits, not an extra filter the alert needs to apply. Getting this wrong means the alert fires on every sweep and gets muted, which is worse than not having it.
40	
41	## Log-sink retention: the deletion-gap enforcement site
42	
43	Application logs, metrics, and traces have no `source` column, no clock tied to a `user_profile` row, and no key a subject-deletion request could reach by cascade — a deleted profile's data can still exist in a log line indefinitely. 05's never-log list (`handoff-04-secrets.md`) and this document's enforcement mechanism (above) are the upstream control — if PII never enters a log, there's nothing in a log to expire — but that only holds for the fields on the list. What's left (chiefly the audit trail: `sub`, scope, opaque record id, timestamp — permitted by design, pseudonymous rather than PII-free) still needs a retention window: `pii-governance.md` § "Retention windows (S5)", the "Audit-log stream" row (clock: the entry's own timestamp; window: proposed, pending a legal ruling; disposal: **expiry at the sink**) names this document's log-sink retention policy as its enforcement site — the citation resolves in both directions. Not a deletion job 04 writes — a retention setting on infrastructure 04 already operates.
44	
45	**Not the same object as the `deletion_log` table.** 05's `deletion_log` (rows written transactionally by `DeleteExpired`/`DeleteProfile`) is a separate thing from the audit-log stream above — it's PII-free by construction, carries no `RetentionClass`, and isn't in 05's retention table at all; nothing here applies to it, and no metric from a `SweepResult` does either. If a retention window is ever wanted for that table, it's a DAO contract change (05's to make), not a sink-retention config value (04's).
46	
47	## What's deliberately not here
48	
49	No SLOs/error-budget policy, no alerting-rule catalog beyond the retention-sweep alert above. A take-home needs the shape of an observability approach and a credible enforcement mechanism for the one hard requirement (never-log); it doesn't need a full SRE runbook.
50	
51	---
52	*AI tooling note: drafted directly by Theo (Sonnet, this session) from `handoff-04-secrets.md` v2's never-log list. Bree's (capability-case) and Callum's (scope-cut) per-deliverable notes reviewed and folded in inline above, per PLAN.md §3.*

1	# Hand-off 02 → 04: Secrets inventory and never-log list
2	
3	Status: **v3.1** — corrects v3's own reasoning on `postgres-superuser` (below): it does *not* stay a Kubernetes Secret after all, since the "bootstrap before Vault exists" argument doesn't hold once Vault is already running independently of Postgres — Theo's finding. Three named exceptions remain, not four. v3 (delivery mechanism changes from raw Kubernetes `Secret` mounts to **Vault Agent Injector, per-ServiceAccount Vault roles**, Warren's ruling, LT-46, 2026-09-13) and everything else are otherwise unchanged. v2's S7 objection turn, F12/F13/F15/F50 fixes still hold — this revision changes *delivery mechanism*, not the inventory or the never-log list. Receiver: Theo Bergman, `../04-infra-devops/`. Written to the project's hand-off standard: 04 should be able to act on this without re-deriving the reasoning in `threat-model.md`, `api-auth-design.md`, or `connector-security.md`.
4	
5	## Assumptions this hand-off makes
6	
7	1. Deployment target is 04's **real Kubernetes cluster, reachable via a plain nginx Ingress + cert-manager on the cluster's shared LoadBalancer** (DNS CNAME to `uplifttech.ddns.net`, public at `https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org` — no tunnel; Warren's implementation go-ahead, 2026-09-12, updated from a design-only reference). **Delivery mechanism (updated 2026-09-13, LT-46): the Vault Agent Injector, not raw Kubernetes `Secret` mounts, for every application-level secret in the inventory below** — a Vault Agent sidecar, added via pod annotation, authenticates to Vault using the pod's own Kubernetes ServiceAccount token (Vault's Kubernetes auth method) and renders each secret to a file on an in-memory (`emptyDir`, `medium: Memory`) volume shared with the application container; nothing application-level is written to a Kubernetes `Secret` object, and therefore nothing application-level reaches etcd's base64-not-encrypted-by-default storage at all — this closes the etcd requirement below by removing the object from etcd entirely, not by enabling etcd encryption at rest. Amber (Vault operator) provisions the KV paths, Vault policies, and Kubernetes-auth roles that make this work; names only are visible in any transcript, never values. Every requirement below is written so it still holds regardless of mechanism; this is the mechanism 04 actually builds to now. **The repository is now public** (`https://github.com/wtvamp/loginid-takehome`) — every requirement in this document that says "never in source or committed config" is now categorical, with no private-repo safety net.
8	
9	**`postgres-superuser` is Vault-delivered after all — v3's reasoning here was wrong, corrected rather than quietly patched over.** v3 argued a Vault Agent sidecar "doesn't help a database's own first-init bootstrap" because it's a pattern for pods already running and authenticating to Vault themselves. That's true of the *sidecar* pattern specifically, but conflates two different things: whether *Vault itself* exists yet (it does — an independent HA Vault, already running on this cluster, not something Postgres hosts or depends on existing) versus whether *this specific pod* has started yet. Vault Agent supports an **init-container** mode for exactly this shape of problem: it runs to completion *before* the main container starts, authenticates via the pod's own ServiceAccount token the same as the sidecar pattern, and renders the credential to a shared volume the main container then reads at its own startup — Postgres's official image already supports `POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE` (a file path) as an alternative to `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` (a literal value), so the init script reading from a Vault-rendered file instead of a `secretKeyRef` env var is a small, well-supported change, not a redesign. There was no actual chicken-and-egg here once Vault's own existence is independent of Postgres's — v3 treated "the database is bootstrapping" and "Vault needs bootstrapping" as the same problem when they aren't.
10	
11	**Postgres gets its own Vault role, `loginid-takehome-postgres`**, reading `postgres-superuser` plus the four existing DB-credential paths (`db-migrator-credential`, `db-runtime-credential`, `issuer-db-migrator-credential`, `issuer-db-runtime-credential`) — five paths, the widest of any role in this system, since the Postgres StatefulSet's init script is what seeds all of them. This is the role deserving the closest look in Helena's policy review, precisely because it's the widest.
12	
13	**Three named exceptions remain Kubernetes Secrets, not Vault-delivered:**
14	
15	- **`postgres-server-tls`** — cert-manager's own `Certificate`/`Secret` mechanism (`threat-model.md` Assumption 9), consumed directly by the Postgres StatefulSet's TLS startup args. cert-manager already *is* a secure, automated, rotation-aware delivery mechanism purpose-built for exactly this (a server needing a TLS keypair) — routing it through Vault instead would duplicate a solved problem, not close a gap.
16	- **`loginid-takehome-tls`** — same reasoning, one level up: the public Ingress's cert-manager-issued certificate, consumed by the Ingress controller directly. Same native, already-secure mechanism; no benefit to re-routing through Vault.
17	- **The ARC GitHub App auth credential** (in the runner namespace, not this application's namespace) — a platform/CI-infrastructure secret belonging to the runner controller itself, not to `api-service`/`idp-connector`/the issuer. It authenticates GitHub's App to provision self-hosted runners; it's outside this application's own trust boundary and namespace, and this hand-off's scope is this application's secrets, not the CI platform's own.
18	
19	The common thread across all three: each is either an already-native, already-secure Kubernetes-integrated mechanism (cert-manager) that Vault would only duplicate, or a platform-level secret outside this application's own namespace/trust boundary — not an ad hoc carve-out, and not "hasn't gotten to it yet."
20	
21	**A fourth item, `qa-client-credential`, is a different category from the three above and stays a Kubernetes Secret for a different reason (ruled 2026-09-13, jointly with Theo Bergman): it's an ops/review-tooling artifact, not an application secret, and structurally cannot use the Vault Agent Injector pattern at all.** The Vault Agent mechanism delivers a secret into a *running pod's filesystem at startup* — `qa-client-credential` is never consumed that way; its only consumption pattern is a human running `kubectl get secret qa-client-credential -o jsonpath=...` directly to manually mint a review token, with no pod in between. There's nothing to inject into. Moving it to Vault would only relocate where a human types a fetch command (`kubectl` → `vault` CLI); it wouldn't close any actual exposure, since the exposure surface either way is "whoever can authenticate as a cluster operator." **Least-privilege confirmation, not just deferred:** checked against `deploy/rbac.yaml` directly — the CI deployer's Role has no `secrets` verbs at all, and no application ServiceAccount (api-service, issuer, idp-connector) has any Secrets-API grant either, since Vault handles their application secrets now. `qa-client-credential`'s actual access boundary is therefore "human operators with direct cluster access," which is Assumption 7's existing operator trust boundary — not a new, unstated exposure this migration should have closed and didn't.
22	2. Service topology is 03's published boundary (`../PLANNING.md`, Service boundaries): two binaries, `cmd/api-service` (Q1 DAO + Q2 REST API) and `cmd/idp-connector` (Q3), env-var configuration only. Variable names below are 03's; two are marked as pending this track's final scheme. **The token-issuing authorization-server function is a mode of `api-service`, not a third binary** — this take-home's scope doesn't justify a standalone authz server, and 03 has published no third `cmd/` entrypoint. **Corrected by the cross-track consistency pass (F13):** "a mode of the same binary" is not "the same Kubernetes Deployment," and the earlier text here was wrong to imply RBAC alone separates them — a Kubernetes Role restricting `get` on a Secret has no effect on a volume already mounted into a container, so if `api-service`'s one Deployment mounted the signing key, every replica (verification path included) would have it on disk regardless of RBAC. **The fix: the issuer runs as a second Deployment of the same `api-service` image**, selected by an environment variable 03 adds to the config surface (e.g. `APP_MODE=issuer` vs. the default, consistent with the surface's env-var-only, no-flags rule), with **its own ServiceAccount that alone mounts the signing-key Secret** (row #2) — the `api-service` Deployment serving verification/search mounts nothing from row #2. This is a request into 03's Deployment/mode surface, not a unilateral change on my part; flag to me if the mode-selection mechanism needs to differ.
23	3. An **external operator agent** — a human or automated actor outside this project, holding cluster-admin or namespace-admin rights on 04's real lab cluster, who applies Kubernetes manifests to it — is a privileged actor outside this service: anyone who can apply a manifest can read any Secret it mounts. Consequence for 04: manifests reference Secrets by name and never carry values; values are set out-of-band. This is a named trust boundary in `threat-model.md` Assumption 7, not a silent assumption; nothing further about who that operator is needs to be re-derived from 04's own files to act on this hand-off. As of this update, 04 has not yet made contact with that operator or deployed anything — `threat-model.md` carries the dated status.
24	4. **Updated 2026-09-12:** with Warren's implementation go-ahead, 04's build stories will deploy to and contact the operator of the real cluster named in Assumption 1 — this is no longer a hypothetical the document describes in the abstract. As of this update, that contact hasn't happened yet; `threat-model.md` Assumption 7 carries the current, dated status and gets updated the moment it changes.
25	
26	## Secrets inventory
27	
28	| # | Secret | Used by | Injection requirement (02 sets it) | Rotation expectation | Delivery mechanism (04's, updated v3: Vault Agent unless named otherwise) | Never-log |
29	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
30	| 1 | Database credentials, carried inside `DB_DSN` | `api-service` | Runtime-injected; never in source, image, or committed config. **Required, not preferred: file mount via a `DB_DSN_FILE` convention** (03 to add alongside `DB_DSN`), because a DSN's embedded password is exposed by `kubectl describe`, crash dumps, and child-process environment exactly as much as any other secret in this table — there is no citation that makes `DB_DSN` a lesser case, so this track is not treating it as one | On credential rotation; service restart or reload acceptable | **Vault Agent sidecar**, rendered to the path `DB_DSN_FILE` reads, on an in-memory volume; `api-service`'s Vault role reads exactly this KV path and nothing else. Env-var `DB_DSN` remains a fallback only until 03 ships the file-based convention | Yes — the DSN contains the password; log the driver name and host at most |
31	| 2 | JWT signing **private** key (RS256/ES256), per `api-auth-design.md` | The issuer Deployment (`api-service` image, `APP_MODE=issuer`) only — **not** the `api-service` Deployment that verifies with the public key; these are two Deployments of one image, not one Deployment doing both (F13 fix) | Runtime-injected as a **file**, never an env var; asymmetric so the verifying Deployment never holds it | Key rotation with a `kid` claim; publish old and new public keys together during overlap; TTL of tokens (5–15 min) bounds the overlap window | **Vault Agent sidecar, only on the issuer Deployment's pod spec** — the verifying Deployment's Vault role has no policy path granting this secret at all (least-privilege at the Vault-policy layer now, in addition to the pod-spec-absence and CI-deployer-Role controls below, which still hold independently of the delivery mechanism): (a) the verifying Deployment's pod spec contains no reference to this secret at all; (b) the CI deployer's Role has no `secrets` verbs on Kubernetes Secrets, and (new) no Vault policy grants it read on this KV path either; (c) provisioned into Vault out-of-band, never through the CI-managed manifest set | Yes |
32	| 3 | JWT **public** key / JWKS | `api-service` middleware | Not a secret; may ship in config or be fetched from the issuer | Follows #2 | Plain `ConfigMap` or fetched URL; listed here so 04 does not over-protect it — not secret, not Vault-delivered | No (but never log full tokens — see below) |
33	| 4 | API caller `client_secret`s (OAuth2 client-credentials) | Authorization server (verifies); callers hold their own copy | **Stored hashed** (Argon2id, same floor as `threat-model.md` Asset 1), never plaintext at rest; issued once at provisioning and not recoverable | Per-client rotation on demand; revoke by deleting the hash | Lives in the issuer's own Postgres database (`oauth_client` table), not a Kubernetes Secret or Vault KV path; 04 delivers only that database's own connection credential (Vault-delivered, same shape as row #1) | Yes |
34	| 5 | Connector's own vendor registration: `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_ID`, `IDP_ABC_CLIENT_SECRET` (and the XYZ pair if a second vendor is configured) | `idp-connector` | Runtime-injected; never in source or committed config; `client_id` is low-sensitivity but travels with the secret and gets the same handling | Per vendor's policy; expect manual rotation | **Vault Agent sidecar**, one KV path per vendor so rotating ABC never touches XYZ; `idp-connector`'s Vault role reads exactly its own vendor paths | Yes for the secret; keep the id out of logs too |
35	| 6 | Vendor `access_token` (returned by our own `/auth` to its authenticated caller, presented as a header on our own `/identity` — updated for F3's caller-holds-the-token model, seam re-run regression fix) | `api-service` holds it briefly between the two calls; `idp-connector` only ever sees it as a caller-presented header on a single `/identity` request, never fetches or holds it between calls itself | **Not a deployed secret** — transient on both sides, never cached, never persisted. Neither `api-service` nor `idp-connector` writes it to a datastore (S3's no-cache ruling, restated for the two-party model in `connector-security.md` §1) | Expires on its own; never persisted across restarts by either party | No `Secret` object and no Vault KV path anywhere in this flow — there is nothing here for 04 to provision | Yes — never, including error paths, on both sides of the exchange |
36	| 7 | Data-encryption key (KEK) for envelope encryption of any TOTP shared secrets | `api-service` (TOTP) only — **not a committed feature of this submission**; `user_credential.method` is modeled as an extensible lookup table (`../05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md`) that supports TOTP as a *possible* value, but no TOTP implementation is planned unless 03 builds one. **The vendor-token-cache use case for this row no longer exists**: S3's red/blue on the connector token lifecycle is closed (`decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`) — no cache by default, and the token is never held by the connector between calls at all under F3's model (row 6), so there is no vendor-token cache for a KEK to encrypt. This row is provisioned only if TOTP materializes — it is a contingency the inventory names in advance, not a live requirement for 04 to build now | Runtime-injected as a file; the KEK never leaves the process that decrypts | Rotation by re-wrapping DEKs; never requires re-hashing passwords (those are hashed, not encrypted) | If this row is ever provisioned: Vault's own Transit engine is a more natural fit than a static KEK on a KV path (Vault already does envelope encryption as a first-class feature) — 04's call if this materializes, the requirement is only that the key exists, is not in source, and isn't a plain KV secret if Transit is available | Yes |
37	| 7a | Credential to reach the external KMS, **only if 04 exercises a non-Vault-Transit KMS alternative in row #7** (a role, API key, or client cert — whichever the chosen KMS uses) | Whichever binary holds the KEK reference (#7) | Same discipline as every other row here: runtime-injected, never in source, never provisioned ad hoc outside this table | Per the KMS provider's own rotation mechanism | Cloud-KMS access is typically workload-identity-based (no static credential to store); if the chosen KMS instead requires a static credential, it's Vault-delivered the same as every other row, kept out of the CI deployer's access the same way row #2's is | Yes |
38	| 8 | TLS serving private keys for `api-service` and `idp-connector` | Ingress / each binary, per 04's termination choice | Runtime-injected; TLS 1.2+ only; certificate validation never disabled in any environment (`connector-security.md` §2) | Per certificate lifetime; automated issuance preferred | **Named exception, stays a Kubernetes `Secret` (`kubernetes.io/tls`), not Vault-delivered** — cert-manager's native mechanism, same reasoning as `loginid-takehome-tls`/`postgres-server-tls` above: Vault would only duplicate a problem cert-manager already solves | Yes |
39	| 9 | End-user's vendor password (`POST /auth` request body) | `idp-connector`, transient | Never persisted, never cached, held only for the vendor call | n/a | Nothing for 04 to deliver; listed so nobody creates a place for it | **Yes — under no circumstance, including stack traces** |
40	| 10 | `api-service`'s own client credential (`client_id`/`client_secret`) toward the issuer, scoped `connector:identity-lookup`/`aud: idp-connector-service` — required for `api-service` to call `idp-connector`'s own authenticated `/auth`/`/identity` (`connector-security.md` §5) | `api-service` (the Deployment serving question 2's API, acting as a caller of the connector on a user's behalf) | Runtime-injected; never in source or committed config — same discipline as row 5's vendor registration, added by the cross-track consistency pass (F15), which found this credential asserted as a requirement in `connector-security.md`/`handoff-03-auth.md` with no inventory row | Same per-client rotation as row 4 (revoke by deleting the issuer-side hash) | **Vault Agent sidecar**, `api-service`'s Vault role reads this path in addition to row #1's — the two are separate KV paths under the same role, not merged, so a policy review can still see them as distinct grants | Yes |
41	| 11 | Postgres bootstrap superuser credential (`postgres-superuser`), plus the four DB-credential paths the same init script seeds (`db-migrator-credential`, `db-runtime-credential`, `issuer-db-migrator-credential`, `issuer-db-runtime-credential`) | Postgres StatefulSet's `initdb` scripts, at first container init | `POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE` (file path, not a literal env var) for the superuser; the init script reads the other four the same way | Manual, rare (bootstrap-only credentials) | **Vault-delivered via init-container mode (corrected, v3.1) — not the sidecar pattern, and not a Kubernetes Secret.** A Vault Agent init container runs to completion before the main Postgres container starts, authenticates via the StatefulSet's own ServiceAccount, and renders all five paths to a shared volume the main container reads at its own startup. Dedicated role `loginid-takehome-postgres`, the widest role in this system (five paths) — the one deserving the closest look in policy review | Yes |
